interconnections between key topics such as artificial intelligence,
personalized learning, machine learning, students, and education. The
red cluster highlights AI’s central role as the network’s core,
surrounded by terms like machine learning, education, and ChatGPT. These
findings reflect a strong focus on the development of AI-based
algorithms and tools to support education. Nevertheless, a research gap
exists in integrating AI with cross-disciplinary contexts such as
psychology and pedagogy. Future studies could explore how AI can model
cognitive and emotional aspects to enhance students’ learning
experiences. The green cluster includes terms such as personalized
learning, students, e-learning, and educational technology, indicating
an interest in using adaptive technologies to personalize the learning
experience. Nevertheless, the long-term impact of personalized learning
on educational equity and inclusion has received limited attention.
Therefore, future research may explore the potential of AI to support
underserved communities and reduce disparities in educational access.
The blue cluster comprises terms like recommendation systems, privacy,
and ethics, reflecting concerns regarding the responsible application of
AI in education. However, ethical issues like algorithmic bias and data
privacy protection have not been explored as extensively as technical
innovations. Future research could expand the development of ethical
frameworks to ensure fair and transparent AI applications in education.
The yellow cluster highlights teaching, curriculum, and teacher topics,
focusing on the interaction between educators and AI-based systems.
While there is interest in the potential of AI to support educators,
research on educators’ perceptions of this technology and their
readiness to integrate it into teaching practices remains limited.
Future studies could focus on developing training programs for educators
to enhance human-machine collaboration in classroom learning. Overall,
this visualization provides insights into the dynamics of AI research
development in personalized learning. While there are several gaps, such
as cross-disciplinary integration, scalability, implementation,
long-term studies, and sustainability, future research should aim at
more inclusive, sustainable, and broadly impactful solutions. This
approach will optimize AI’s transformative educational potential to
create more adaptive and responsive learning systems globally. Fig.
5 presents a bibliometric visualization generated using VOSviewer,
illustrating the current research trends related to the application of
Artificial Intelligence (AI) in personalized learning. This
visualization depicts the relationships between key terms and the
distribution of research from 2019 to 2024. Yellow indicates
increasingly popular themes in recent years, such as ChatGPT and
contrastive learning, while green highlights topics developed earlier
but remain relevant, such as machine learning and personalized learning.
The term “artificial intelligence” occupies a central position in the
network, with extensive connections to various other terms, reflecting
AI’s role as the core of this research. Furthermore, its linkage to
terms like ethics, privacy, e-learning, and students underscores
attention to multidisciplinary challenges, including ethical concerns
regarding AI use, data privacy protection, and the integration of
educational technologies to support more personalized and adaptive
learning. Although research has made significant progress, several gaps
remain to be addressed. For instance, privacy and data security issues
have not received adequate attention. Similarly, accessibility and
inclusion for underserved communities remain significant challenges.
Moreover, the long-term impact of AI on academic performance and
students’ well-being has yet to be explored in depth. The relationship
between AI and the teacher’s role in curriculum design and creating more
adaptive teaching methods also requires further attention in future
studies. Prospects in this field include the development of ethical
frameworks that ensure the responsible use of AI, integrating AI with
local needs to expand educational access, and using AI-based
technologies, such as ChatGPT, to enhance content personalization and
teaching methods. Research on these dimensions is expected to yield
innovative, inclusive, and sustainable solutions that can transform the
global education ecosystem. Thus, these findings answer (RQ2) by
demonstrating that over the past five years, AI has served as a
supporting technology and has become a key driver in creating
student-centered, equitable, and globally responsive learning systems.
3.3. Challenges, opportunities, and benefits of AI-driven personalized
learning In order to address the research question (RQ3) concerning the
challenges, opportunities, and benefits of Artificial
Intelligence-driven personalized learning, Table 5 summarizes key
findings from recent research across various educational contexts. Table
5. Challenges, opportunities, and benefits of AI personalized learning
systems. Table 5 provides a comprehensive overview of the challenges,
opportunities, and benefits of implementing AI-based personalized
learning across various educational institutions, including the need for
adequate technological infrastructure, educator training, and data
privacy and AI bias issues. Furthermore, the opportunities arising from
AI implementation include the personalization of educational content to
meet individual student needs, improved accessibility, and the use of
big data analytics to enhance educational resource management. The
benefits include increased student motivation following AI technology,
improved learning outcomes through real-time feedback, and a more
adaptive and personalized learning experience. In addressing the
research question (RQ3), Table 5 successfully presents concrete evidence
from a range of studies demonstrating that, despite technical and
ethical challenges, AI can enhance educational quality through more
effective personalization and increased student engagement in the
learning process. 3.4. Impact of AI-based personalized learning on
student performance AI-based learning has proven more effective in
improving student performance than traditional methods, which often
provide uniform content for all students with slow feedback. This
results in students with slower learning speeds falling behind, while
students with higher capabilities are not adequately challenged to
develop their potential. In contrast, AI-based learning can tailor the
learning experience to the needs and abilities of each student, as shown
in Fig. 6.

Download: Download high-res image (286KB) Download: Download full-size
image Fig. 6. Core components of AI technologies and a personalized
learning framework. As depicted in Fig. 6, the AI-based framework
facilitates a more individualized and measurable approach. AI analyzes
student learning patterns in real-time, provides instant feedback, and
adjusts content according to each individual’s understanding level and
learning style. The four key elements of the AI framework for
personalized learning are: a. Customized learning paths involve AI
automatically adjusting the learning trajectory based on a student’s
mastery of a specific topic. For example, several students struggling
with basic mathematical concepts will be provided additional material
until sufficient understanding is reached before progressing to the next
level. b. Competency-based learning allows students to proceed to the
following material after fully mastering the previous content. Unlike
conventional methods, this ensures a deep understanding of concepts,
where student progress is often determined by a rigid schedule rather
than individual comprehension levels. c. AI-based Systems that Maintain
Dynamic Student and Teacher Profiles track each student’s learning
preferences, strengths, and weaknesses. This data enables the alignment
of teaching methods with the student’s learning style. For instance,
visual learners will receive more graphics and diagrams, while verbal
learners will benefit from text-based explanations. d. Personalized
feedback mechanisms enable learners to identify and correct their
mistakes quickly, thanks to AI technology. Unlike traditional methods,
where teacher feedback is often delayed until long after completing
assignments, AI provides immediate correction and guidance. Recent
studies (Demartini et al., 2024; Gligorea et al., 2023) indicate that
implementing machine learning in adaptive learning systems has improved
student understanding by up to 25 % compared to conventional learning
methods. For example, adaptive learning algorithms used in mathematics
lessons adjust the difficulty level of questions based on student
responses, so more proficient students are given more complex
challenges, while those needing additional support receive more
straightforward questions with supplementary explanations. The core AI
technologies in personalized learning include: a. Machine Learning
Models analyze patterns in student performance and learning behaviors,
enabling predictions about areas where students need additional support
or learning resources. b. Natural Language Processing (NLP) allows the
AI system to understand and respond to student queries in natural
language, creating a more interactive learning experience by providing
instant feedback and accelerating material comprehension. c. Data
analysis and visualization involve presenting student progress in clear
and accessible formats, such as charts or interactive maps. These tools
assist educators in making precise decisions by providing actionable
insights. For example, they enable teachers to identify students who
require special attention, allowing for the effective implementation of
targeted teaching strategies to address individual learning needs. d.
Adaptive Learning Algorithms adjust the difficulty of materials based on
the student’s understanding and development level. In summary, AI
technology and the personalized learning framework offer innovative
approaches to addressing various challenges in traditional learning,
particularly in responding to research questions (RQ4). Leveraging
cutting-edge technologies such as machine learning models, natural
language processing (NLP), and adaptive learning algorithms, this
framework can deliver a more efficient, relevant, and focused learning
experience. Compared to conventional methods, AI-based learning deepens
students’ understanding of concepts and fosters active engagement in the
learning process. This advantage highlights the potential of AI in
creating a more effective and impactful educational ecosystem.
3.5. Limitations, implications, and future directions on AI in
personalized learning In addressing the research question (RQ5), which
explores AI’s limitations, implications, and future directions in
personalized learning, Table 6 presents a comprehensive review
highlighting technical challenges and the need for further research to
optimize AI usage. Table 6. Limitations, implications, and future
Directions of AI-Driven personalized learning. Table 6 provides concrete
evidence supporting the response to the research question (RQ5),
detailing the limitations, implications, and future potential of AI
applications in personalized learning. At the same time, Table
5 presents a clear overview of previous studies on AI-driven
personalized learning. Several key challenges identified include
barriers in technology integration, dependency on high-quality data, and
the lack of trials in diverse educational contexts, indicating that
AI-based learning systems still face significant obstacles in terms of
sustainability and scalability. Although AI is not without its
shortcomings and occasionally makes errors, the implications arising
from its application reveal its tremendous potential to revolutionize
the educational landscape. With its ability to tailor learning content
to individual needs, enhance student engagement, and provide prompt and
accurate feedback, AI demonstrates its role as a tool capable of
enriching the learning experience. This technology supports a more
personalized approach and offers flexible solutions to address various
student ability levels, making it a crucial contributor to creating
inclusive and adaptive education systems. Further technological
advancements and in-depth research on implementing AI across different
educational environments are necessary to realize this potential. Future
directions emphasize the importance of overcoming technical limitations,
refining recommendation algorithms, and exploring emerging AI
applications, such as ChatGPT, Intelligent Tutoring Systems, and more
sophisticated AI-based adaptive learning systems. 4. Discussion
4.1. Challenges in AI-driven personalized learning systems The
integration of AI into personalized learning systems presents various
challenges, as identified in several studies (Kanchon et al.,
2024; Naseer et al., 2024; Tapalova & Zhiyenbayeva, 2022; Wang, 2023).
According to research by (Huang et al., 2023), there was no significant
difference in learning motivation between the control and experimental
groups despite using AI-based recommendation systems. The study
(Tapalova & Zhiyenbayeva, 2022) emphasizes technological infrastructure
and teacher training as prerequisites for successfully adopting
Artificial Intelligence in Education (AIEd). Similarly, findings (Xu et
al., 2023) highlight ethical concerns related to student data collection
and analysis, complicating privacy guarantees and accuracy. These
challenges reflect the complexities of transitioning from traditional
learning models to AI-based models, requiring attention to
technological, ethical, and practical barriers. Compared to previous
research findings (Baltezarević & Baltezarević, 2024; Kanchon et al.,
2024; Kucirkova & Leaton Gray, 2023; Ogata et al., 2024; Tang et al.,
2020; Xiao & Yi, 2021), it is evident that while AI-based learning
systems have garnered attention, their integration is still hindered by
infrastructure gaps and resistance from both educators and students. The
lack of adequate teacher training and robust systems to manage
personalized learning content are recurring issues (Rawas, 2024; Schmid
et al., 2022; Villarreal, 2023). Research by (Alsobhi & Alyoubi, 2019)
also supports this, as they found limited experimental results focused
solely on computer science students, without broader application across
various educational contexts. Addressing these challenges is crucial to
making AI systems more scalable and effective in diverse settings.
Future research should explore innovative approaches to address these
challenges. As suggested by (Wang, 2023), integrating the Internet of
Things (IoT) and providing training for both teachers and students could
offer a stronger framework for AI applications. Additionally, further
investigation into the ethical implications of AI use in education and
its potential biases, as proposed by (Baba et al., 2024), is necessary
to ensure the fair implementation of this technology. 4.2. Opportunities
in AI-driven personalized learning systems Despite existing challenges,
each finding presents substantial potential and opportunities for
utilization, particularly in AI-driven personalized learning, which is
vast and transformative. The study conducted by (Tapalova &
Zhiyenbayeva, 2022) focuses on the potential of AI to tailor educational
content to align with individual student needs, enabling learners to
study according to their unique style, pace, and requirements. Similarly
(Xiao & Yi, 2021), argue that AI-based systems can design personalized
training plans tailored to students’ career objectives. With AI-based
systems, such as those used in flipped classrooms, there is an
opportunity to enhance student engagement and motivation, as noted by
(Huang et al., 2023). The ability to adapt the learning experience to
individual preferences and abilities represents a significant
opportunity to improve learning outcomes and make personalized education
more interactive and meaningful. These opportunities align with previous
research findings by (Bekmanova et al., 2021; Deng et al., 2024; Kanchon
et al., 2024; Ogata et al., 2024; Wang, 2023; Xu et al., 2023), which
explore how personalized training models can enhance the flexibility and
effectiveness of courses. AI’s ability to analyze vast amounts of data
and provide immediate feedback also offers unique advantages in
enhancing student engagement and developing essential 21st-century
skills, as shown by (Tapalova & Zhiyenbayeva, 2022). The integration of
AI with educational psychology, as suggested by (Wei et al., 2021),
allows for more accurate resource recommendations, helping reduce
cognitive load and aligning learning materials with students’
capabilities. In order to leverage these opportunities, future research
should focus on the practical application of AI skills, such as
developing adaptive algorithms, recognizing student learning behavior
patterns, and integrating cross-disciplinary data to create more
holistic learning models (Ayanwale et al., 2024; Bauer et al., 2023).
Additionally, it is crucial to explore how AI can support collaborative
learning by enabling more dynamic interactions between students,
teachers, and technology (Kim, 2024; Tan et al., 2022). Future research
must address ethical and privacy concerns, particularly in collecting
and analyzing student data. This includes developing policies that
ensure data security and algorithm transparency to ensure its use is
acceptable to all parties involved. Finally, training educators to
optimally leverage AI technology becomes a crucial aspect that cannot be
overlooked. This is essential in equipping teachers with the necessary
technical and pedagogical skills to manage AI-based learning systems.
Furthermore, research should explore the scalability of these
opportunities, especially in underrepresented educational settings, and
AI’s role in promoting educational equity, as noted by (González-Rico &
Lluch Sintes, 2024). 4.3. Benefits of AI-driven personalized learning
systems The benefits of using AI in personalized learning systems are
evident in several studies showing substantial improvements in student
engagement, performance, and overall learning outcomes. In particular,
research by (Wei et al., 2021) found that AI-based personalized learning
can significantly reduce cognitive load while improving the accuracy and
adaptability of learning materials. According to (Xu et al., 2023),
AI-based systems improved student performance by 95.32 %, indicating the
effectiveness of personalized feedback in enhancing educational
outcomes. Furthermore, studies conducted by (Alsobhi & Alyoubi, 2019)
observed that AI-based personalized learning systems helped improve
learning outcomes, especially for students with diverse learning styles,
such as those with dyslexia. These benefits are consistent with previous
research, which shows that AI-based systems can enhance strategic
thinking, problem-solving skills, and student motivation (Annuš & Kmeť,
2024; Baba et al., 2024; Huang et al., 2023; Ruwe & Mayweg-Paus,
2023; Tang et al., 2020). For instance, a study by (Huang et al., 2023)
highlighted that AI integration supports a more inclusive and adaptive
learning approach, facilitating the provision of materials tailored to
individual preferences. This research underscores the tremendous
potential of AI to accelerate educational transformation in both
developed and developing countries, focusing on effectiveness,
efficiency, and equity in education. Similarly (Ruwe & Mayweg-Paus,
2023), noted that students trust AI-based feedback systems more than
traditional methods, contributing to enhanced trust and motivation among
learners. Research should focus on refining AI-based systems to maximize
their benefits. For instance, studies could investigate integrating AI
with human intelligence more effectively, balancing personalized
learning with social interaction, as suggested by (Kucirkova & Leaton
Gray, 2023). Additionally, exploring the long-term impacts of AI would
be valuable for student retention, career readiness, and creativity,
ensuring that these systems are effective in the short term and support
lifelong learning and adaptability. 4.4. Limitations of AI-based
personalized learning systems The limitations of AI-based personalized
learning systems are diverse, yet many stakeholders share concerns
regarding the readiness and implementation of such systems across
various educational sectors. For example (Huang et al., 2023), noted
that AI-based personalized learning systems often overlook external
factors, such as the learning environment and social interactions. These
limitations may restrict the system’s effectiveness outside the digital
learning environment, even though this technology can potentially
enhance student motivation and engagement. However, these limitations
are not only technical but also involve the preparedness of educational
institutions and the skills of educators in adopting such technology.
Other research, such as that conducted by (Alsobhi & Alyoubi, 2019; Xu
et al., 2023), noted that AI systems might face challenges integrating
existing curricula or relying heavily on high-quality data. A lack of
data or poor integration may hinder the system’s ability to provide a
personalized experience that effectively meets deep learning needs.
Additionally, technical issues, such as an inability to address diverse
learning styles or the need for better educator training, were
highlighted (Mokmin, 2020; Zhang, 2022). This suggests that AI systems
should be more adaptable, and educators must be sufficiently trained to
use these systems effectively. Moreover, a study by (Bekmanova et al.,
2021) highlighted the difficulties in consistently evaluating
personalized learning models, particularly within higher education. This
raises concerns regarding the scalability and generalization of AI-based
learning models, especially in higher education environments (Govea et
al., 2023; Kuleto et al., 2021). The challenges of reliability and data
representation for personalized learning models, as discussed by (Ogata
et al., 2024), further emphasize the need for more comprehensive
datasets to support adaptive AI systems. 4.5. Implications Despite the
existing limitations, AI-based personalized learning systems present
significant implications for enhancing the educational experience. For
example (Huang et al., 2023), found that AI-based recommendation systems
could improve student engagement and motivation, particularly in flipped
classroom settings, demonstrating how technology can create more
flexible and adaptive learning environments, allowing students to access
materials according to their needs before in-person sessions. Similarly
(Tapalova & Zhiyenbayeva, 2022), emphasized that AI in education
technology can adapt learning content to meet individual needs,
enhancing student engagement and motivation. Regarding broader
educational reforms (Xiao & Yi, 2021), highlighted that AI’s potential
to personalize education could produce the best graduates from higher
education, enabling them to explore professional development
opportunities, including career paths in the future. This aligns with
the findings of (Alsobhi & Alyoubi, 2019), who noted that personalizing
learning based on learning styles or specific learning disabilities,
such as dyslexia, could significantly improve learning outcomes.
Furthermore, integrating AI into the educational process can help
personalize learning more effectively, as AI can address various
learning preferences and challenges that traditional methods may
overlook. AI also has the potential to foster a collaborative,
student-centered learning environment, but to realize this potential,
educator training is essential to help them understand, integrate, and
effectively use this technology in the learning process. For example,
research focused on physical education by (Mokmin, 2020) found that
personalized virtual coaches could help increase engagement and
productivity, demonstrating how AI can be applied in various educational
settings. Therefore, it is crucial to develop further AI-based tools and
applications that focus on developing learning content and enhancing
educators’ pedagogical skills in using this technology. 4.6. Future
directions Moving forward, several areas where AI-based personalized
learning systems can be further developed to maximize their impact.
According to (Tang et al., 2020), there is a need to develop adaptive
learning models that are relevant to various educational contexts,
particularly in fields such as STEM or educational games. This suggests
that future research should focus on creating AI systems that are not
only personalized but also contextually relevant, ensuring that the
unique learning needs of students in diverse fields such as science,
technology, engineering, mathematics, and engineering (Xu & Ouyang,
2022). Additionally, integrating AI with other technologies, such as big
data and the Internet of Things (IoT), is still in its early stages and
requires further optimization (J. Li et al., 2023; Paramesha et al.,
2024). Research by (Xiao & Yi, 2021) proposes that future AI
applications should be more comprehensive, integrating a broader range
of data sources to improve the accuracy and effectiveness of
personalized learning models. Research on the long-term effects of AI in
education is also crucial, as highlighted by (Zhang, 2022), to
understand how this technology impacts learning outcomes over time. AI
integration’s ethical and technical challenges, particularly concerning
student data privacy, must also be addressed (Akgun, S., & Greenhow,
2022). According to (Guettala et al., 2024), it is crucial to balance
the benefits of AI with ethical considerations when handling student
data. Future research should explore methods to safeguard data privacy
while still offering personalized learning experiences. Furthermore,
subsequent studies are advised to focus on enhancing the adaptability of
AI systems to various teaching methods, ensuring that AI enhances human
intelligence rather than replacing it. This is especially important
given the rapid advancement of technology, as noted by (Ruwe &
Mayweg-Paus, 2023), who caution that shifts in perceptions of AI must be
closely monitored. 5. Limitations of the review This systematic review
is subject to several methodological limitations. First, the search was
restricted to articles indexed in the Scopus database and published
between 2019 and 2024, without inclusion of other indexing services such
as Web of Science, PubMed, Google Scholar, or Semantic Scholar. As such,
potentially relevant studies outside the Scopus coverage particularly
from across regional, non-Western, or K–12 contexts may have been
inadvertently excluded. Second, only English-language peer-reviewed
journal articles were included, whereas conference papers, grey
literature, and articles in press were excluded to ensure publication
quality. This exclusion may limit the scope of emerging or exploratory
research which is often first presented in non-journal formats or
regional languages. Third, the defined five-year timeframe (2019–2024)
was selected to capture recent developments in AI-enabled personalized
learning; however, this may exclude foundational studies published prior
to this interval. Fourth, the focus on empirical research excluded
conceptual or theoretical articles, thereby omitting insights that might
provide deeper understanding of ethical, pedagogical, or policy-related
dimensions of AI in education. Finally, most of the included studies
reported short-term outcomes (e.g., engagement, satisfaction), with
limited tracking of long-term impacts such as equity, sustainability,
and cognitive development. Ethical concerns, including data privacy,
algorithmic bias, and stakeholder readiness were acknowledged in several
studies but rarely interrogated in depth. Future systematic reviews
should expand database coverage beyond Scopus, incorporate non-English
literature, and consider diverse research designs (including grey
literature and preprints) to provide a more inclusive and globally
representative synthesis of AI-driven personalized learning research.
6. Conclusion This literature review uses the PRISMA framework to
identify the developments and applications of AI-driven personalized
learning over the past five years (2019–2024). Based on RQ1, a
systematic analysis uncovered 25 key articles covering technologies such
as machine learning, deep learning, natural language processing, and
intelligent tutoring systems. Research distribution indicates a
dominance of the Computer Science field, contributing 24 %, followed by
contributions from the social sciences, engineering, medicine, and other
multidisciplinary areas. Afterward, RQ2 explores emerging trends in
AI-based learning through data visualization, with key clusters
including (1) the development of AI-based tools, such as ChatGPT, to
support education; (2) personalized learning using adaptive
technologies; (3) concerns over ethics and privacy in AI usage; and (4)
interaction between educators and AI-based systems. Based on RQ3, the
findings reveal the challenges, opportunities, and benefits of AI-based
learning. Despite technical and ethical barriers, applying AI under
appropriate guidelines can improve educational quality, particularly in
personalizing learning and developing 21st-century skills. In response
to RQ4, AI-based learning is more effective than traditional methods.
These systems can enhance student performance and learning styles on an
individualized basis, ensuring more profound and measurable progress.
However, RQ5 identifies several limitations, such as a lack of
generalization in studies, a focus on short-term outcomes, and
challenges in integrating AI into traditional curricula. Nevertheless,
AI has the potential to foster a more flexible and collaborative
learning approach, improving student engagement and providing rapid,
accurate feedback. Attention to ethical issues and educator training
remains critical. To maximize these benefits, future research should
address these limitations by refining adaptive learning algorithms,
expanding studies across diverse educational environments, and
developing ethical policies that support AI as a tool to assist, rather
than replace, human effort since AI can sometimes make mistakes, such as
providing inaccurate or contextually irrelevant answers. With careful
and sustained implementation, AI holds the potential to create an
inclusive, adaptive, and meaningful learning environment for the future
of education. CRediT authorship contribution statement Aprilla

(Artificial intelligence in personalized learning: A global systematic

李宗諺 G5) ” New Information - What this handout is about You’ve likely
heard of AI tools such as ChatGPT, Microsoft Copilot, Perplexity, or
others by now. These tools fall under a broad, encompassing term called
generative AI that describes technology that can create new text,
images, sounds, video, etc. based on information and examples drawn from
the internet. Some AI tools are free or offer free tiers, while others
require a subscription and payment. In this handout, we will focus on
potential uses and pitfalls of generative AI tools that generate text.
Before we begin: Stay tuned to your instructor Instructors’ opinions on
the use of AI tools may vary dramatically from one class to the next, so
don’t assume that all of your instructors will think alike on this
topic. Consult each syllabus for guidance or requirements related to the
use of AI tools. If you have questions about if/how/when it may be
appropriate to use generative AI in your coursework, be sure to seek
input from your instructor before you turn something in for a grade. You
are always 100% responsible for whatever writing you chose to turn in to
an instructor, so it pays to inquire early. Note that when your
instructors authorize the use of generative AI tools, they will likely
assume that these tools may help you think and write—not think or write
for you. Keep that principle in mind when you are drafting and revising
your assignments. You can maintain your academic integrity and employ
the tools with the same high ethical standards and source use practices
that you use in any piece of academic writing. What is generative AI,
and how does it work? Generative AI is an artificial intelligence tool
that allows users to ask it questions or make requests and receive quick
written responses. It uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to analyze vast
amounts of textual data to determine patterns in words and phrases.
Detecting patterns allows LLMs to predict what words may follow other
words and to transform the content of its corpus (the textual data) into
new sentences that respond to the questions or requests. Using complex
neural network models, LLMs generate writing that mimics human
intelligence and varied writing styles. The textual data used to train
the LLM has been scraped from the internet, though it is unclear exactly
which sources have been included in the corpus for each AI tool. As you
can imagine, the internet has a vast array of content of variable
quality and utility, and generative AI does not distinguish between
accurate/inaccurate or biased/unbiased information. It can also
recombine accurate source information in ways that generate inaccurate
statements, so it’s important to be discerning when you use these tools
and to carefully digest what’s generated for you. That said, the AI
tools may spark ideas, save you time, offer models, and help you improve
your writing skills. Just plan to bring your critical thinking skills to
bear as you begin to experiment with and explore AI tools. As you
explore the world of generative AI tools, note that there are both free
and paid versions. Some require you to create an account, while others
don’t. Whatever tools you experiment with, take the time to read the
terms before you proceed, especially the terms about how they will use
your personal data and prompt history. Prompting In order to generate
responses from AI tools, you start by asking a question or making a
request, called a “prompt.” Prompting is akin to putting words into a
browser’s search bar, but you can make much more sophisticated requests
from AI tools with a little practice. Just as you learned to use Google
or other search engines by using keywords or strings, you will need to
experiment with how you can extract responses from generative AI tools.
You can experiment with brief prompts and with prompts that include as
much information as possible, like information about the goal, the
context, and the constraints. You could experiment with some fun
requests like “Create an itinerary for a trip to a North Carolina
beach.” You may then refine your prompt to “Create an itinerary for a
relaxing weekend at Topsail Beach and include restaurant
recommendations” or “Create an itinerary for a summer weekend at Topsail
Beach for teenagers who hate water sports.” You can experiment with
style by refining the prompt to “Rephrase the itinerary in the style of
a sailor shanty.” Look carefully at the results for each version of the
prompt to see how your changes have shaped the answers. The more you
experiment with generative AI for fun, the more knowledgeable and
prepared you will be to use the tool responsibly if you have occasion to
use it for your academic work. Here are some ways you might experiment
with generative AI tools when drafting or exploring a topic for a paper.
Potential uses Brainstorming/exploring the instructor’s prompt
Generative AI can help spark ideas or categories for brainstorming. You
could try taking key words from your topic and asking questions about
these ideas or concepts. As you narrow in on a topic, you can ask more
specific or in-depth questions. Based on the answers that you get from
the AI tool, you may identify some topics, ideas, or areas you are
interested in researching further. At this point, you can start
exploring credible academic sources, visit your instructor’s office
hours to discuss topic directions, meet with a research librarian for
search strategies, etc. Generating outlines AI tools can generate
outlines of writing project timelines, slide presentations, and a
variety of writing tasks. You can revise the prompt to generate several
versions of the outlines that include, exclude, and prioritize different
information. Analyze the output to spark your own thinking about how
you’d like to structure the draft you’re working on. Models of genres or
types of writing If you are uncertain how to approach a new format or
type of writing, an AI tool may quickly generate an example that may
inform how you develop your draft. For example, you may never have
written—a literature review, a cover letter for an internship, or an
abstract for a research project. With good prompting, an AI tool may
show you what type of written product you are aiming to develop,
including typical components of that genre and examples. You can analyze
the output for the sequence of information to help you get a sense of
the structure of that genre, but be cautious about relying on the actual
information (see pitfalls below). You can use what you learn about the
structures to develop drafts with your own content. Summarizing longer
texts You can put longer texts into the AI tool and ask for a summary of
the key points. You can use the summary as a guide to orient you to the
text. After reading the summary, you can read the full text to analyze
how the author has shaped the argument, to get the important details,
and to capture important points that the tool may have omitted from the
summary. Editing/refining AI tools can help you improve your text at the
sentence level. While sometimes simplistic, AI-generated text is
generally free of grammatical errors. You can insert text you have
written into an AI tool and ask it to check for grammatical errors or
offer sentence level improvements. If this draft will be turned into
your instructor, be sure to check your instructor’s policies on using AI
for coursework. As an extension of editing and revising, you may be
curious about what AI can tell you about your own writing. For example,
after asking AI tools to fix grammatical and punctuation errors in your
text, compare your original and the AI edited version side-by-side. What
do you notice about the changes that were made? Can you identify
patterns in these changes? Do you agree with the changes that were made?
Did AI make your writing more clear? Did it remove your unique voice?
Writing is always a series of choices you make. Just because AI suggests
a change, doesn’t mean you need to make it, but understanding why it was
suggested may help you take a different perspective on your writing.
Translation You can prompt generative AI tools to translate text or
audio into different languages for you. But similar to tools like Google
Translate, these translations are not considered completely “fluent.”
Generative AI can struggle with things like idiomatic phrases, context,
and degree of formality. Transactional communication Academic writing
can often involve transactional communication—messages that move the
writing project forward. AI tools can quickly generate drafts of polite
emails to professors or classmates, meeting agendas, project timelines,
event promotions, etc. Review each of the results and refine them
appropriately for your audiences and purposes. Potential pitfalls
Information may be false AI tools derive their responses by reassembling
language in their data sets, most of which has been culled from the
internet. As you learned long ago, not everything you read on the
internet is true, so it follows that not everything culled and
reassembled from the internet is true either. Beware of clearly written,
but factually inaccurate or misleading responses from AI tools.
Additionally, while they can appear to be “thinking,” they are literally
assembling language–without human intelligence. They can produce
information that seems plausible, but is in fact partly or entirely
fabricated or fictional. The tendency for AI tools to invent information
is sometimes referred to as “hallucinating.” Citations and quotes may be
invented AI responses may include citations (especially if you prompt
them to do so), but beware. While the citations may seem reasonable and
look correctly formatted, they may, in fact, not exist or be incorrect.
For example, the tools may invent an author, produce a book title that
doesn’t exist or incorrectly attribute language to an author who didn’t
write the quote or wrote something quite different. Your instructors are
conversant in the fields you are writing about and may readily identify
these errors. Generative AI tools are not authoritative sources.
Responses may contain biases Again, AI tools are drawing from vast
swaths of language from their data sets–and everything and anything has
been said there. Accordingly, the tools mimic and repeat distortions in
ideas on any topic in which bias easily enters in. Consider and look for
biases in responses generated by AI tools. You risk violating academic
integrity standards When you prompt an AI tool, you may often receive a
coherent, well written—and sometimes tempting—response. Unless you have
received explicit, written guidance from an instructor on use of AI
generated text, do not assume it is okay to copy and paste or paraphrase
that language into your text—maybe at all. See your instructor’s
syllabus and consult with them about how they authorize the use of AI
tools and how they expect you to include citations for any content
generated by the tool. The AI tools should help you to think and write,
not think or write for you. You may find yourself violating the honor
code if you are not thoughtful or careful in your use of any AI
generated material. The tools consume personal or private information
(text or images) Do not input anything you prefer not to have widely
shared into an AI generator. The tools take whatever you put in to a
prompt and incorporate it into its systems for others to use. Your ideas
may be changed unacceptably When asked to paraphrase or polish a piece
of writing, the tools can change the meaning. Be discerning and thorough
in reviewing any generated responses to ensure the meaning captures and
aligns with your own understanding. A final note Would you like to learn
more about using AI in academic writing? Take a look at
the modules in Carolina AI Literacy and the UNC Libraries’ Student Guide
to AI Literacy. Acquainting yourself with these tools may be important
as your thinking and writing skills grow. While these tools are new and
still under development, they may be essential tools for you to
understand in your current academic life and in your career after you
leave the university. Beginning to experiment with and develop an
understanding of the tools at this stage may serve you well along the
way. Note: This tip sheet was created in July 2023. Generative AI
technology is evolving quickly. We will update the document as the
technology and university landscapes change. - ” (Generative AI in
Academic Writing by 李宗諺 G5) ” My Idea - This handout from UNC-Chapel
Hill provides a balanced framework for using Generative AI (GenAI) in
academia. It moves beyond simple “dos and don’ts” to explain the
mechanics of Large Language Models (LLMs) and how to maintain academic
integrity while leveraging their power. 🧠 How Generative AI Works
Generative AI uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to process massive
amounts of internet data. Pattern Recognition: It doesn’t “know” facts;
it predicts the next word in a sequence based on statistical patterns.
Neural Networks: These allow the AI to mimic human styles and tones,
creating the illusion of intelligence. The Training Corpus: Because the
data is scraped from the internet, the AI reflects the internet’s mix of
high-quality info, misinformation, and bias.

🛠️ Strategic Uses for Students The guide suggests several ways to use AI
as a “thinking partner” rather than a replacement for your own voice:

⚠️ Critical Pitfalls to Avoid Using AI in an academic setting comes with
“hallucinations” and ethical risks: Fabricated Information: AI can
generate plausible-sounding lies. It does not distinguish between fact
and fiction. Fake Citations: One of the most dangerous traps; AI often
invents authors, titles, and quotes that look real but do not exist.
Academic Integrity Risks: Copying/pasting AI text without permission or
citation is often considered a violation of the Honor Code. Data
Privacy: Anything you type into a prompt becomes part of the AI’s
training data. Never input private personal info or proprietary
research.

💡 The Golden Rule: Consult Your Instructor The most important takeaway
is that policies vary by class. * Check the Syllabus: Don’t assume a
“yes” in English class means a “yes” in Biology. Instructor Intent: Most
professors see AI as a tool to help you think and write, not to think or
write for you. Responsibility: You are 100% responsible for the accuracy
and integrity of the work you turn in, regardless of how it was
produced. As the handout notes, the landscape of 2026 is moving fast.
Developing AI Literacy—the ability to critically analyze and ethically
use these tools—is becoming as essential as basic research skills. - ”
(summary by 張庭睿 G5) ” My Idea - This qualitative study examines how
post-graduate L2 (English as a second language) writers navigate the
“confusing terrain” of Generative AI (GenAI) during their transition
into higher education. By interviewing 21 students from Mainland China
studying in Hong Kong, the researchers explored the tension between
student agency and institutional policies. 🧠 Conceptual Framework:
Distributed Agency The study is built on the idea of distributed agency,
which suggests that when a student uses AI, the capacity to “act” is
shared between the human and the machine. Empowerment vs. False
Empowerment: While students feel empowered by AI’s ability to summarize
and polish text, the researchers warn of “false empowerment.” Without
Critical Digital Literacy (CDL), students may unknowingly let algorithms
dominate their voice or introduce biases. Ideological Tensions:
Universities often hold a “disempowerment” ideology (fearing plagiarism
and loss of skill), while students often hold an “empowerment” ideology
(viewing AI as a vital assistant).

📊 Key Research Findings The study utilized a Preference Indication Task
and focus groups to answer two main research questions: 1. How L2
Writers Perceive GenAI (RQ1) The “Human-in-the-Loop” Model: Students
view themselves as the “leaders” and AI as a “supportive assistant.”
They feel responsible for the final ideas, even if AI helps with the
phrasing. Practical Affordances: Students integrated AI for linguistic
support, explaining complex concepts, and generating outlines. It
provides a “confidence boost” regarding grammatical accuracy. Critical
Awareness Gaps: Despite using the tools, many students showed a lack of
deep understanding regarding how algorithms work, often trusting AI
outputs without rigorous verification. 2. Reaction to Instructor
Policies (RQ2) The participants’ preferences for AI policies varied
based on their desire for autonomy and fairness.

💡 Implications for Education The study suggests that institutions
should move away from binary “ban or allow” mentalities and instead:
Foster Critical Digital Literacy: Teach students how AI works so they
aren’t “falsely empowered” by biased algorithms. Encourage Transparency:
Adopt “AI Declaration” models that allow flexible use while maintaining
accountability. Reform Assessment: Design tasks that recognize the
collaborative nature of human-AI writing rather than trying to police it
through unreliable detection software. The researchers conclude that for
L2 writers, AI is not just a tool for “cheating,” but a necessary
scaffold for navigating complex academic environments—provided they are
taught to use it critically and transparently. - ” (summary by 張庭睿
G5) ” This article reframes writer’s block in academia not as a lack of
willpower, but as a systemic workflow failure. It argues that the
“fragmentation tax”—the mental energy wasted switching between
disconnected research, citation, and writing tools—is the primary cause
of academic paralysis. Here is a summary of the strategic solutions
presented to overcome these hurdles: 1. Eliminating the “Fragmentation
Tax” Academic writing is often stalled because researchers juggle
separate apps for PDFs, notes, citations, and drafting. The Solution:
Use integrated ecosystems (like SciPub+) that unify literature
organization and manuscript drafting in one space. The Result: By
removing the logistical friction of “tool-hopping,” cognitive energy is
preserved for actual deep thinking and argument building. 2. Combating
“Blank Page Anxiety” via Segmentation Staring at a massive project (like
a thesis) creates cognitive overload. The Solution: Break the manuscript
into manageable sections and use structured prompts. The Oracle Method:
Instead of “Write the Introduction,” ask specific questions like, “What
background sets the context for your research?” This turns writing into
a series of answers rather than a daunting creative feat. 3. Ending
“Research FOMO” with Knowledge Flow Writers often stall because they
fear their information is outdated or they are missing a crucial new
paper. The Solution: Implement proactive discovery tools (like
SciDigest) that deliver curated research updates directly to the writer.
The Result: Constant input prevents “knowledge stagnation” and builds
the confidence needed to push through complex sections without
second-guessing. 4. Overcoming Stylistic Hesitation Uncertainty about
academic tone leads to self-editing loops that kill momentum. The
Solution: Use iterative, AI-driven language tools (like Text Tune) that
suggest disciplinary norms and formal restructuring. The Result: Writers
learn stylistic conventions as they go, reducing the “mental tax” of
sentence-level crafting in future drafts. 5. The Three Pillars of a
Sustainable Workflow To prevent future blocks, the article suggests a
self-renewing loop: Guided Structure: Always know your next step before
opening the laptop. Ongoing Discovery: Regularly replenish ideas with
fresh research. Iterative Refinement: Polishing prose in focused
sessions rather than obsessing over every word during the first draft.
The Bottom Line Writer’s block is a signal to restructure. By moving
away from “willpower” and toward an integrated, prompt-driven workflow,
academic writing changes from a struggle against a blank screen into a
guided process of “bringing your words home.” ” (summary by 張庭睿 G5) ”
My Idea - This research summary by Irina Tzoneva (Douglas College)
explores the practical and linguistic differences between
student-written and AI-assisted essays, while also investigating the
human side of AI integration in the classroom. 🔍 Research Questions &
Methodology The study followed 13 undergraduate students through two
writing tasks: one in a controlled environment (no AI) and one at home
(AI encouraged). The Goal: To see if instructors can spot the difference
and to understand student motivations and challenges. The Analysis:
Included linguistic complexity tests (readability formulas) and
double-blind reviews by experienced faculty.

📊 Key Findings: Linguistic “Fingerprints” The study found distinct
“styles” that separate AI-assisted work from organic student writing:

🗣️ Student Perspectives: Five Core Themes Interviews with the
participants revealed a complex relationship with the technology: AI as
a Support Tool: Students view AI as a “sophisticated editor” for grammar
and clarity, not as a ghostwriter for their actual arguments. Strategic
Use: It is used selectively—mainly when a student is “stuck” or needs a
quick outline. Ethical Anxiety: Students worry about “unintentional
plagiarism” and the accuracy of AI-generated facts. Institutional
Ambiguity: A major pain point was the inconsistency between professors.
Students are often confused by conflicting rules in different classes.
Need for AI Literacy: There is a strong desire for formal training
(workshops/modules) on how to use these tools ethically and effectively.

💡 Conclusion The study suggests that while AI can mimic academic
writing, it lacks the organic structural logic of a human writer.
However, because overall “quality” scores were similar between both
groups, instructors cannot rely on “bad writing” as a sign of AI use.
Tzoneva emphasizes that the solution isn’t better detection, but better
communication: clear institutional policies and “AI Literacy” education
are essential to reduce student anxiety and maintain academic
integrity. - ” (summary by 張庭睿 G5) ” My Idea - This study, presented
at the ICRES 2024 International Conference, explores how university
students (specifically EFL graduate students) navigate the use of AI in
academic writing. It seeks to balance the undeniable efficiency of these
tools with the ethical “lines” that must not be crossed. Here is a
summary of the findings categorized by the study’s three core research
questions: 1. Why Students Use AI (Motivation) The research identified
three primary drivers for AI adoption: Speed & Efficiency: AI
dramatically shortens the “pre-writing” phase. It helps students process
large volumes of information and reach a “fixed concept” or outline
faster, which is crucial when facing multiple deadlines. Overcoming
Writer’s Block: Students use AI as a brainstorming partner when they are
“stuck” or don’t know how to continue a sentence. Enhancing Quality: For
EFL (English as a Foreign Language) students, AI acts as a sophisticated
bridge to “more academic” sounding prose, correcting grammar and
suggesting advanced vocabulary. 2. Ethical “Red Lines” and Concerns
Despite the benefits, students expressed significant dilemmas regarding
academic integrity: The Plagiarism Trap: There is a constant fear that
AI-generated text lacks originality or that it will be flagged by
detection software. Information Inaccuracy: Students noted that AI often
fails to understand specific contexts and can produce “hallucinations”
or incorrect data. Loss of Originality: A major internal conflict for
students is the feeling that the work is no longer “purely” theirs. They
combat this by insisting on “putting their own voice” and critical
thinking back into the AI’s suggestions. The Dependency Risk: There is a
concern that AI might “dominate” the writing process, turning the
student into an editor rather than a writer. 3. Reliability: When to Use
(and Avoid) AI The study defines clear boundaries for where AI excels
and where it fails:

Key Takeaways & Recommendations The study concludes that while AI is a
“multipurpose tool” that enhances productivity, it cannot replace the
human-in-the-loop. Human Control: The author must maintain ownership of
the content and use AI to assist, not replace. Verification: Writers
should manually verify all information through credible databases (like
Semantic Scholar or digital libraries) because AI is often unreliable
for factual literature reviews. Institutional Regulation: Especially for
undergraduate students, universities need clear policies to ensure AI is
used to hone skills rather than bypass the learning process. Citation:
Afifah. (2024). Using Artificial Intelligence (AI) for Academic Writing:
How Not to Cross the Line. Proceedings of ICRES 2024. - ” (summary by
張庭睿 G5) ” My Idea - AI literacy” AND “higher education” AND “future
of work”​“Artificial Intelligence” AND “employability skills” AND
university - ” (Future Workforce & AI Literacy by 李宗諺 G5) ” My Idea -
This article emphasizes that AI literacy is no longer a niche technical
skill but a fundamental requirement across all academic disciplines. It
argues that universities must shift from teaching about AI to teaching
students how to collaborate with it responsibly. 🧩 What is AI Literacy?
AI literacy is defined as the ability to understand, guide, and
critically question artificial intelligence. EDUCAUSE identifies four
essential dimensions that form this new educational foundation:
Technical Knowledge: Understanding the basic mechanics of how AI
functions. Evaluative Judgment: The ability to determine the quality,
accuracy, and relevance of AI outputs. Practical Integration: Knowing
how to use AI tools effectively within a specific professional or
academic context. Ethical Awareness: Recognizing bias, privacy concerns,
and the legal implications of AI.

💼 Impact on Career Readiness The definition of “employability” is
shifting from deep specialization to human-system collaboration. The
Skills Gap: There is a growing disconnect between traditional academic
knowledge and the operational reality of AI-enabled workplaces. From
Users to Co-designers: Literacy empowers students to interpret machine
data and identify algorithmic bias, making them more valuable to
employers who need adaptable professionals. Reduced Anxiety: Studies
show that students with high AI literacy have better academic outcomes
and lower stress when using automated tools.

🛠️ 4 Practical Steps for Universities The article outlines a roadmap for
institutions to modernize their curricula: Cross-Disciplinary
Integration: AI should be critiqued in Literature, used for forecasting
in Business, and modeled for ethics in Engineering. Focus on
Collaboration: Education should prioritize “prompt design” and iterative
testing—learning by doing rather than by lecture. Embedded Ethics:
Ethical reflection on privacy and accountability should be a thread
throughout every program, not a separate “one-off” module. Workforce
Alignment: Universities should offer microcredentials and skill-based
projects that reflect the “new currency” of the job market.

❓ Questions for the Modern Educator To successfully transition, faculty
must ask: Are we measuring factual recall or the ability to collaborate
with systems? Do our assessments encourage students to critically
question automated tools? How can we model AI literacy in our own
teaching practices? Final Word The “Iceberg” metaphor suggests that we
have only seen the surface of AI’s potential. Universities are now
responsible for shaping the cognition and ethics of a workforce that
will spend their entire careers thinking alongside machines. - ”
(summary by 張庭睿 G5) ” My Idea - In this reflective summary of the
Graduate Employability Forum, the author moves beyond the “jobpocalypse”
hype to analyze how AI is fundamentally restructuring the shape of the
workforce and the definition of professional expertise. The core
argument is that the “pyramid” model of hiring is evolving into a
“diamond” model, requiring a shift from simple AI literacy to deep AI
fluency.

📉 From Automation to Augmentation The author highlights a critical
distinction between two ways organizations implement AI: Automation
vs. Augmentation. Using data from a recent Stanford study on software
development, the results are counterintuitive: The Automation Trap: When
AI is asked to complete a full task (e.g., “write this entire report”),
productivity actually declined by 18% due to the heavy burden of human
checking and fixing errors at the end. The Augmentation Edge: When
humans stay in control of the workflow and delegate specific “sub-steps”
to AI (e.g., data cleaning), productivity increased by 24%. “AI isn’t
replacing human judgement. It’s making the absence of judgement more
visible.”

💎 The “Diamond-Shaped” Workforce For decades, companies used a Pyramid
Model: many entry-level roles performed routine tasks while learning the
“tacit knowledge” of the profession. AI is turning this into a Diamond
Model: Compressed Entry: Routine, programmable tasks (data entry, basic
reporting) are being automated. Cognitive Loading: Entry-level roles are
now “broader and less scaffolded.” New graduates are being asked to
solve complex anomalies on day one—tasks previously reserved for
mid-career professionals. The Risk: If organizations eliminate “junior”
tasks, they destroy the training ground where professional judgment is
formed, leading to a long-term talent crisis.

🎓 Beyond Literacy: The Need for AI Fluency The author argues that
universities must move past teaching “how to use the tool” (Literacy) to
teaching “how to lead the workflow” (Fluency).

🚀 A Call for “Deep Design” The takeaway for higher education is that
“tinkering at the edges” of the curriculum is insufficient. Authentic
Learning: Move away from “curriculum in boxes” (isolated subjects)
toward tasks that mimic the integrated, AI-augmented reality of modern
work. Industry Partnerships: Universities and employers must co-design
the “developmental pathways” of the future. They must figure out how a
graduate can develop “expert judgment” if the “beginner tasks” are gone.
The author concludes that the transition is a shared design challenge.
Industry must resist the short-term lure of full automation, and
universities must accelerate the integration of professional judgment
into every level of the degree. - ” (summary by 張庭睿 G5) ” My Idea -
This study analyzes how artificial intelligence (AI) skills influence
the employability of university graduates, specifically focusing on
alumni from the National University Toribio Rodríguez de Mendoza of
Amazonas (UNTRM-A). Below is a summary of the key findings, the
“AI-Employability Gap,” and the study’s conclusions.

1. Core Findings: AI as a Career Catalyst

The research established a direct link between AI proficiency and
professional success. Graduates who actively use AI tools—particularly
generative AI like ChatGPT, Google Gemini, and Microsoft Copilot—report
the following: Better Job Alignment: Those with advanced AI skills are
more likely to work in sectors directly related to their majors.
Increased Productivity: Daily or frequent users of AI report
significantly higher work efficiency and quality. Competitive Edge: AI
skills are now viewed as a “key differentiating factor” in a crowded
labor market. ## 2. The Current State of AI Knowledge Despite the high
interest in AI, the study identified several critical trends: The
Generational Gap: Recent graduates (2021–2025) possess much higher AI
literacy than those who graduated before 2010. Self-Taught Dominance:
Most graduates learned AI through personal initiative or online courses,
rather than formal university curriculum. The “Intermediate” Plateau:
Most professionals are at an intermediate level; very few consider
themselves “experts” in specialized AI (e.g., data analysis or
automation).

3. Challenges and Barriers

The study highlighted a significant disconnect between graduation and
job-market readiness: Widespread Insecurity: Only 16.9% of graduates
feel “very prepared” for the AI-driven labor market. Formal Education
Lag: A low percentage of graduates received formal AI training during
their degrees, leading to a call for universities to modernize.
Skepticism & Ethics: While trust is generally high, a segment of the
workforce remains skeptical due to concerns over privacy, job
displacement, and algorithmic bias.

4. Final Recommendations

The authors conclude that AI should no longer be an elective or niche
subject. To ensure a fair and effective transition into the digital age,
they recommend: Transversal Integration: AI should be taught across all
majors (Medicine, Law, Education, etc.), not just Engineering.
Continuous Training: Universities must offer alumni specialized programs
and workshops to bridge the generational gap. Soft & Hard Skill Synergy:
While practical experience remains the top factor in getting hired
(39.9%), AI is the primary tool that amplifies that experience. Key
Statistic: 95.6% of graduates expressed an active interest in taking
further AI courses to strengthen their professional performance. - ”
(summary by 張庭睿 G5)

“     New Information - While artificial intelligence (AI), exemplified

there is a correlation between the frequency of use of ChatGPT and
Plagiarism, but causality was not found. Besides, a moderator effect of

manner. ” (Generative AI in higher education: Seeing ChatGPT through

services, AI-powered essay mills, and sophisticated plagiarism tools
that challenge everything we thought we knew about academic honesty. But
here’s the fascinating paradox: the same artificial intelligence
technology that’s enabling widespread academic dishonesty is also
providing educators with unprecedented tools to detect, prevent, and
even reimagine how we approach academic integrity. The Scale of Modern
Academic Dishonesty Beyond Traditional Cheating Gone are the days when
academic dishonesty meant glancing at a neighbor’s test or copying from
SparkNotes. Today’s cheating economy operates with the sophistication of
a Fortune 500 company, complete with: Contract cheating services that
employ thousands of freelance writers globally AI-powered essay
generators that can produce original content in minutes Sophisticated
plagiarism tools that can rewrite existing content to avoid detection
File-sharing networks with millions of completed assignments Exam-taking
services that use advanced technology to complete online tests The

academic dishonesty have increased by 340% since 2012, with the most

it didn’t just democratize artificial intelligence – it fundamentally

launch: 89% of students knew about ChatGPT 48% had used it for at-home
assignments 22% admitted to using it for graded assessments 64% reported
using it for various academic tasks by the end of the academic year What
makes this particularly challenging for educators is the quality and
sophistication of AI-generated content. Unlike traditional plagiarism,
which involved copying existing text, AI tools create original content
that can be remarkably difficult to distinguish from human writing. ”
(The $89 Billion Cheating Economy: How AI Detection Tools Are
Revolutionizing Academic Integrity by 李宗諺 G5) “The explosive
proliferation of Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) has posed an
unprecedented existential challenge to traditional evaluation systems in
higher education. In response to this wave, the higher education sector
is undergoing a profound transformation, shifting from a focus on
technological containment toward an assessment governance paradigm.
Understanding the characteristics of detectors is the foundational
prerequisite for utilizing such tools to optimize educational
assessment; however, their effectiveness within authentic educational
contexts remains insufficiently explored. To address this research gap,
the study constructs three large-scale ecological datasets—StuTask,
StuThesis, and DataCode—comprising over 280,000 authentic samples of
student coursework, academic theses, and engineering code. A systematic
evaluation was conducted on 13 mainstream detectors, covering both
commercial and open-source models, across multiple dimensions including
overall performance, task complexity, disciplinary variations, and
adversarial robustness. The results indicate that while detectors
achieve acceptable performance on long-form theses, they exhibit
systematic failures in engineering code and short-form coursework tasks.
Due to the formulaic nature of technical writing, STEM disciplines are
subject to significant algorithmic bias. Furthermore, robustness tests
reveal the extreme vulnerability of current detection tools, as a hybrid
editing strategy can enable up to 88% of AI-generated content to evade
detection successfully. These findings suggest that existing detection
technologies are inadequate to support high-stakes educational
assessments. In the future educational trajectory of ‘embracing AI,’
AIGC detectors should function as reference metrics within the
assessment system, serving to quantify the depth of human–AI
collaboration across distinct disciplinary logical frameworks. Detection
technology must evolve toward the optimization of ‘logical innovation
recognition,’ thereby establishing a robust academic integrity defense
line that is truly resilient within the future ecosystem of human–AI
symbiosis.” (Trusting AI to detect AI? A systematic evaluation of the
reliability and robustness of current AIGC detection tools for student
academic work by 李宗諺 G5)

” (The Degradation of Critical Thinking and Fundamental Writing Skills
by 李宗諺 G5) “With the growing integration of artificial intelligence
(AI) in education, understanding its cognitive implications has become
increasingly important. This study examines how university students’ AI
dependence influences their critical thinking, exploring cognitive
fatigue as a mediating mechanism and information literacy as a
moderating factor. Data were collected from 580 Chinese university
students, and a moderated mediation model (PROCESS Model 8) was tested.
Results indicated that greater AI dependence was associated with lower
levels of critical thinking, with cognitive fatigue partially mediating
this relationship. Information literacy buffered the negative impact of
AI dependence on critical thinking but also amplified cognitive fatigue
when AI reliance was high. These findings extend theories of cognitive
offloading and automation bias by revealing the dual role of information
literacy in AI-supported learning. Practical implications include the
need for digital literacy programs and instructional designs that manage
cognitive fatigue in AI-integrated education.” (Learners’ AI dependence
and critical thinking: The psychological mechanism of fatigue and the
social buffering role of AI literacy by 李宗諺 G5) “The rapid
integration of artificial intelligence (AI) into higher education
presents both cognitive opportunities and risks. Guided by a partial
application of the I-PACE framework (operationalizing
affective–cognitive reactions and executional tendencies), this study
examines how perceived AI intelligence (PIAI) is associated with
university students’ critical thinking (CT) and whether focused
immersion and AI dependency mediate this association. Two questions are
addressed: (a) Is PIAI directly associated with CT in AI-supported
academic tasks? (b) Do focused immersion and AI dependency serve as
mediators, including a sequential pathway? Cross-sectional survey data
were collected from 698 Chinese undergraduates using validated scales.
Confirmatory factor analysis and structural equation modeling with
bias-corrected bootstrapped indirect effects were used to test the
hypothesized relationships. Results indicate a positive direct
association between PIAI and CT; focused immersion also shows a positive
association with CT and operates as a positive indirect pathway. In
contrast, a negative sequential pathway emerges whereby higher PIAI is
linked to greater focused immersion, which increases AI dependency, and
higher dependency is associated with lower CT. These findings delineate
an attentional route (beneficial immersion) and a reliance route
(detrimental dependency), clarifying when perceived capability supports
versus undermines CT. The study extends I-PACE to educational-AI
settings and informs course and policy design emphasizing
verification-oriented engagement to sustain critical inquiry while
mitigating reliance risks.” (Outsourcing thinking to AI? Focused
immersion, AI dependency, and the double-edged impact on critical
thinking by 李宗諺 G5) “It has been described as “a gamechanger” that
will prove a challenge in universities and schools. Though GCSE and

acquiring the knowledge and skills they need.” (This article is more

Information - Potential Bias in AI AlgorithmsStudies have shown
significant bias in GPT (generative pre-trained transformers; e.g.,
ChatGPT) against non-native English speakers. For example, one study
shows over half of non-native English writing samples were misclassified
as AI generated (while the accuracy for native English speakers was
nearly perfect). Part of the problem is that GPT detectors are
programmed to recognize language that is more literary and complex as
more “human.” Thus, writers not using such language are more likely to
be categorized as using AI-generated content and could be unjustly
flagged for AI plagiarism. Non-English-speaking students may be falsely
accused of cheating, which can undermine their academic career and
damage their psyches. When assessing the use of non-native English
speakers, it might be best not to use GPT detectors as assessment tools
until the detectors have gone through a more comprehensive evaluation.
They can be more safely employed as educational aids, helping students
become better writers. -    
https://education.illinois.edu/about/news-events/news/article/2024/10/24/ai-in-schools–pros-and-cons
” (Potential Bias in AI Algorithms by 李坤憲 G6) “     New Information -
Academic MisconductCheating and plagiarism are, as mentioned, chief
among the AI concerns raised by educators. If AI is used to complete
assignments or exams or write papers, it is unfair to the students who
don’t cheat, and it undermines the education and learning process for
those who do cheat. If students learn to cheat and take shortcuts in
classrooms, what kind of citizens will they make when they are finished
with their education? Measures need to be in place to ensure that AI is
not being used unethically. -    
https://education.illinois.edu/about/news-events/news/article/2024/10/24/ai-in-schools–pros-and-cons”
(Academic Misconduct by 李坤憲 G6) ” My Idea - Relying more and more on
AI may reduce the teacher-to-student interactions and relationships and
take away from the social-emotional aspects of learning. If those
interactions diminish, students’ social skills and interpersonal
development will suffer. Teachers need to be aware of this and take care
to identify and respond to the social and emotional needs of their
students. However, on the flip side, automating administrative tasks
such as lesson planning, grading, and maintaining student records should
free up teachers’ time to spend more time building relationships with
students and fostering their social and emotional growth. Doing so has
proven to result in many benefits, including better grades and higher
college enrollment rates. That said, a recent survey showed that only 22
percent of students—an all-time low—believe that their teachers try to
understand their lives outside of school. Schools can use AI to
facilitate the relationships between teachers and students—but they must
make an intentional effort to do so. It’s a touchpoint to be aware of
and stay on top of. - ” (Reduced Human Interaction by 賴怡安 G6) ” New
Information - The MIT Study: AI and Your Brain AI tools are everywhere
now, and they help us write faster. But a new study from MIT shows a
problem: relying too much on AI might make us lose our ability to think
and remember. Researchers studied 54 people in three groups: those using
AI, those using Google, and those using only their brains. They used a
brain-scan tool called EEG to see how the brain works during writing.
What did they find? The “brain-only” group had the most brain activity.
The AI group had the weakest. Even worse, when the AI group stopped
using the tool and tried to write by themselves, their brains stayed
“quiet.” They couldn’t remember what they wrote and didn’t understand
the topic well. “Metacognitive Laziness” This is called “metacognitive
laziness.” It happens when we let AI do the hard thinking for us. Over
time, we might stop learning how to create our own ideas. The Lesson AI
is not bad, but we shouldn’t trust it blindly. To keep our brains sharp,
we must keep thinking, questioning, and reflecting while we use these
tools. - ” (How does AI affect people , MIT do experiment to observe it!
by 吳中睿 G6) ” New Information - The real question is, can you use AI
in education to encourage critical thinking and promote learning? Katzin
said the key is never the tool itself, but how teachers and students use
it—to replace thinking or to ignite it? To give a man a fish or to teach
him how to fish?
https://www.gvm.com.tw/article/125879?srsltid=AfmBOorZWGN_6Kfk7VZQzt0H22V1_lJxgfniMbVa4y0YEl8Ko1DV82v5 -
” (Unable to think or think better by 李羿鴻 G6) “     My Idea - If

significant bias in GPT (generative pre-trained transformers; e.g.,
ChatGPT) against non-native English speakers. For example, one study
shows over half of non-native English writing samples were misclassified
as AI generated (while the accuracy for native English speakers was
nearly perfect). Part of the problem is that GPT detectors are
programmed to recognize language that is more literary and complex as
more “human.” Thus, writers not using such language are more likely to
be categorized as using AI-generated content and could be unjustly
flagged for AI plagiarism. Non-English-speaking students may be falsely
accused of cheating, which can undermine their academic career and
damage their psyches. When assessing the use of non-native English
speakers, it might be best not to use GPT detectors as assessment tools
until the detectors have gone through a more comprehensive evaluation.
They can be more safely employed as educational aids, helping students
become better writers. - ” (Potential Bias in AI Algorithms by 賴怡安
G6) ” My Idea - If you don’t cross-check against reliable sources, that

human blind spots. Cultural assumptions. Skewed examples. Missing
perspectives - ” (not reliable sources by 賴怡安 G6) ” My Idea -
Plagiarism is another sleeper issue. Even when AI doesn’t copy directly,

similarity. And over time, something subtler creeps in. Skill erosion.

factual errors. It’s honestly infuriating to read. AI is supposed to be

submission full of mistakes, give it a low grade, and then the student
shamelessly comes to argue for more points. They keep pestering me and

have been a concern for as long as AI has been around. People are wary
about what personal data is collected and how it is used—and whether
they have any knowledge of or control over how it is used. Many voice
concerns about how carefully their data is stored and how protected it
is from being leaked. Other worries include having private and sensitive
information viewed by others, having false or misleading information
disseminated, and the increasing ease others have in accessing others’
personal data. Broadly speaking, risks are involved in data collection,
data processing, data dissemination, and invasion (encroaching into an
individual’s personal space, choices, or activities).-

“     My Idea - AI provides dynamic support that adjusts difficulty
based on the learner’s proficiency, transforming unattainable challenges
into achievable milestones.   -     ” (Personalized Scaffolding by
陳宥穎 G7) “     New Information -

The research defines AI-based interactive scaffolding (AIIS) through
“Contingency,” meaning support is adjusted based on a “dynamic
assessment of learners’ current abilities.” In a university context,
this ensures that AI doesn’t just give answers but provides the exact
level of guidance needed to keep the student in the “Zone of Proximal
Development.

資料來自：The effects of artificial intelligence-based interactive
scaffolding on secondary students’ speaking performance, goal setting,

English -     ” (Dynamic Contingency by 陳宥穎 G7) ” New Information -
